Note: In state 1, transition n1x3 goes to destination 1 which with 3 states and leaving state 1, the others are [0,2], so D=1 means state 2. And n1x2 goes to destination 3... wait.

Let me re-read: "With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2."

So for state 1 with 3 states: D=0 → state 0, D=1 → state 2.
- n1x0: D=1 → state 2? No wait: "n1x0 1" means destination index 1, which maps to state 2. But in the champion: --n1x0 1... that means going to "the other state at index 1" which is state 2.

Wait, I'm reading this wrong. The destination D is an index into the list of OTHER states. For state 1 with 3 total states, the others are [state 0, state 2]. So D=0 → state 0, D=1 → state 2.

In the champion state 1:
- n1x0: D=1 → state 2 ✓ (going to state 2)
- n1x1: D=1 → state 2 ✓
- n1x2: D=3... wait --n1x2 3 --c1x2 1. D=3 is out of range for 3 states! Unless... 

Oh wait, I see: "--n1x2 3" might mean the number of the next transition index is 3? No... "--n1x2 D" where D is destination. But with 3 states leaving state 1, D can only be 0 or 1.

Actually wait - I'm re-reading more carefully. "--n1x2 3" - is 3 a valid destination? With 3 total states and leaving state 1, the other states are [0, 2] which means D can be 0 or 1... D=3 doesn't make sense.

Hmm, but this is the champion machine that was already evaluated and got 9808.96. Maybe the format allows D to be the actual state index (not relative)? Let me re-read the spec.

"--n<I>x<K> D : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only. With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2."

So D=3 is invalid for 3 states. But this machine ran... maybe the simulator ignores invalid values or wraps around? Either way, I should copy it verbatim as instructed.

For my new machines, I'll be careful to use valid D values.

- The champion uses: S0=GoToColor(blue=3), S1=Repulsion, S2=Exploration

- Top 3 performers all share the same basic 3-state structure with GoToColor(blue)+Repulsion+Exploration
- 2-state machines with GoToColor were worst performers

- Exploit: tweak rep gain (4.5→4.0, 5.0), rwm (40→50, 30), probabilities on transitions

**Destination index analysis for the champion structure (3 states):**
- Leaving state 0: others=[1,2], D=0→state1, D=1→state2
- Leaving state 1: others=[0,2], D=0→state0, D=1→state2
- Leaving state 2: others=[0,1], D=0→state0, D=1→state1

Champion state 0 uses D=1 (→state2), D=0 (→state1), D=1 (→state2), D=1 (→state2) ✓
Champion state 1 uses all D=1 (→state2) - all transitions go to exploration state ✓
Champion state 2 uses D=1 (→state2?? no, D=1 from state2 → state1=Repulsion) and D=0 (→state0=GoToColor) ✓

So state 2 (Exploration): D=1→state1(Repulsion), D=0→state0(GoToColor) - makes sense, when on gray go to GoToColor, when many neighbors go to Repulsion.

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 3 results:**
- Champion (9593.11): GoToColor(blue) + Repulsion(4.8) + Exploration(rwm=45), with specific transition structure
- The 3-state GoToBlue+Repulsion+Exploration topology is clearly dominant
- rep1=4.8, p1x0 threshold=3, w1x0=15, rwm2=45 seem to be the best params so far
- Machines with Stop state or FlockCenter were worst performers

**Key structure of champion:**
- S0 (GoToColor blue): 4 transitions → all going to S1 or S2
  - sees blue (C=7,l=3): → S2 (D=1)
  - on black (C=0): → S1 (D=0)  
  - on gray (C=1): → S2 (D=1)
  - on white (C=2): → S2 (D=1)
- S1 (Repulsion 4.8): 4 transitions → all to S2 (D=1)
  - neighbors>3 (C=3,p=3): stay repelling via S2?... wait
  - Actually leaving S1, D=0→S0, D=1→S2
  - n1x0 D=1 → S2, n1x1 D=1 → S2, n1x2 D=1 → S2, n1x3 D=1 → S2
- S2 (Exploration rwm=45): 2 transitions
  - on gray (C=1, p=0.35): → S2? No D=1→S1
  - neighbors>7 (C=3, p=7): → S0 (D=0)

Wait, let me re-read: leaving S2, others=[S0,S1], D=0→S0, D=1→S1
- n2x0 D=1: → S1 (Repulsion) when on gray
- n2x1 D=0: → S0 (GoToColor) when many neighbors

So: Explore → Repulsion (when on gray), Explore → GoToColor (when many neighbors >7)

This is counterintuitive but works! The machine essentially: GoToBlue → then repels when sees blue or is on black/gray/white, and exploration transitions to repulsion when on gray and to GoToColor when many neighbors.

**Exploitation plan:**
- Tweak rep1 (4.8→5.0, 4.6, 4.2)
- Tweak rwm2 (45→50, 55, 35)
- Tweak p1x0 threshold (3→4, 2)

- Tweak p2x0 (0.35→0.3, 0.4)

**Exploration plan:**
- Try rep1=4.8 with different w values
- 4-state version
- Different rwm values
- Try combining with ZigZag instead of Exploration

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 4:**
- Champion unchanged at 9593.11 - no improvement this round
- Best performers cluster around 9623-9678, all using the same 3-state GoToBlue+Repulsion+Exploration topology
- Key parameter trends from best performers:
  - rep1=4.8 is consistently good
  - w1x0=12 (slightly tighter sigmoid) performs well (9623)
  - rwm2=45-55 range is good
  - p2x0=0.3-0.4 range
  - 3-transition s1 (removing one neighbor condition) also performs well (9650)
  - Adding random transition (C=5, p=0.02) to s0 helps (9666)
- Worst: 4-state machines, adding extra transitions to s2, rep=4.6

**Strategy for Round 5:**
- Survival: copy champion exactly
- Exploitation: combine best features (w1x0=12, rwm2=50, p2x0=0.3-0.4, random transition in s0)
- Cross-breed top performers: mix w1x0=12 from #1 with rwm2=50 from #2
- Exploration: try different neighbor thresholds, different repulsion timing

**Key insight:** The structure works because:
- S0 (GoToBlue): moves toward blue walls, transitions to S1 when on black (arrived!), transitions to S2 for gray/white
- S1 (Repulsion): spreads robots in target zone, transitions to S2 (exploration)
- S2 (Exploration): random walk, transitions to S1 on gray, back to S0 when many neighbors

Let me be very careful with the format - every state needs `--n<I>`, every transition needs destination `--n<I>x<K> D`, condition `--c<I>x<K> C` and its parameters.

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 5:**
- New champion: 9480.74 with rwm2=70 (higher exploration randomness helps!)
- Clear trend: higher rwm2 is better (70 > 55 > 50 > 45)
- w1x0=12 consistently better than w1x0=15
- rep1=4.8 is the sweet spot
- p2x0=0.28-0.35 range for gray-floor transition in exploration state
- w2x1=10.9907 (neighbor threshold in exploration) seems fixed

**Key parameters to explore:**
- rwm2: try 80, 90, 100 (push higher)
- p2x0: try 0.2-0.3 range
- w1x0: try 10, 8 (even tighter sigmoid)
- p1x2 (gray floor in repulsion): try 0.35, 0.3
- w2x1: try different values

**Worst to avoid:**
- Adding extra transition to s2 (n2x2) - hurts performance
- p1x0=2 with w1x0=10 - too low threshold

**Plan:**
- 1 survival copy
- 6 exploitation: vary rwm2 (80,90,100), p2x0, w1x0
- 7 exploration: try different topologies carefully

For exploration, I'll try:
- Champion with rwm2=80/90/100
- Champion with p2x0=0.2
- Champion with w1x0=10 but keeping p1x0=3
- Different w2x1 values

- p0x1=0.08 (black floor in s0) is better than 0.115
- p2x0=0.15 is the sweet spot (0.05 too low, 0.12 bad, 0.2 ok)
- p2x1=7 is best, but 6 with lower p2x0 also works

**Final round strategy - concentrate on most promising directions:**
1. Survival: exact copy
2. Try p0x1=0.05, 0.06, 0.1 (explore around 0.08)
3. Try p0x1=0.08 with p2x0=0.12, 0.1 (cross best params)
4. Try p0x1=0.08 with p2x1=6, 8
5. Try p0x0=0.5, 0.65 (blue-color detection in s0)
6. Try p2x0=0.15 with p0x1=0.08 and w1x3=12
7. Try p0x2=0.15 (gray floor in s0)

**Avoid:** p2x0=0.05 or 0.12, p2x1=6 with p2x0=0.15, w1x0≠12, rep1≠4.8

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 10 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.51 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
